CE Noticias Financieras English May 13, 2021 Thursday Copyright 2021 Content Engine, LLC. All Rights Reserved Copyright 2021 CE Noticias Financieras All Rights Reserved Length: 1572 words Body When China's government ordered contraceptive devices to be placed on women in the Muslim community in the Xinjiang region, Qelbinur Sedik pleaded with them to dispense it. He was almost 50 years old, he told the officials. He had respected the government-imposed child limit and had only one daughter. It was useless. The workers threatened to take her to the police if she kept resisting, she said. He then agreed to go to a government clinic where with a metal forceps a doctor placed an intrauterine device (IUD) on him to prevent him from getting pregnant. She stood still crying throughout the process. "I felt like I was no longer a normal woman," Sedik remarked with a lump in her throat as she recounted the terrible experience of 2017. "Like they're taking something from me." In a large part of China, the authorities are encouraging women to have more children to try to avoid a demographic crisis as a result of a declining birth rate. But in the Xinjiang region, China is forcing them to have fewer children, reinforcing their control over Muslim ethnic minorities and attempting to undertake a demographic transformation that will diminish their population over the generations. According to reports from a renowned researcher, Adrian Zenz, along with those of The Associated Press, as the use of invasive birth control procedures has increased, birth rates in that region have fallen in recent years. This is part of a vast and repressive Communist Party campaign for social redesign bent on removing any alleged obstacles to its government; in this case, ethnic separatism. Over the past few years, the party, led by its leader, Xi Jinping, has been tasked with putting Uighurs and other Central Asian minorities very strongly in Xinjiang by interning hundreds of thousands of them in prison camps and prisons. The authorities have placed this region under strict surveillance, sent their residents to work in factories and moved children to boarding school. Although the authorities have said birth control procedures are voluntary, interviews with more than a dozen Uighurs, Kazakhs and other Muslims in Xinjiang, as well as an analysis of official statistics, government announcements and reports in the state media reveal attempts at coerciveness by the Communist Party of China to impede the reproductive rights of the community. Authorities forced women to use an IUD or sterilize therself. Government officials sent their home to live with them as they recovered for the purpose of detecting signs of nonconformity. One woman mentioned having to endure the touching of her watchman. If they had too many children or rejected contraception proceedings, they would be credited with heavy fines or, even worse, to be confined to a detention camp. In these fields, women were at risk of even more abuse. Some women who were previously arrested argue that they were made to take medications to interrupt their menstrual cycle. A woman claimed to have been raped in one of these camps. For human rights defenders and Western authorities, largely due to attempts to stop the population growth of Muslim minorities, government repression in Xinjiang amounts to crimes against humanity and genocide. In January, the Trump administration was the first to classify these severe measures as genocide; one of the main reasons was the imposition on reproductive issues. In March, Biden's government confirmed this designation. Sedik's experience, narrated in The Guardian and other media, served as the basis for the US government's decision. "It was one of the most detailed and compelling first-person stories we saw," said Kelley E. Currie, who served as U.S. ambassador and participated in government debates. "He helped put a face on the chilling statistics we were seeing." Beijing has accused its opponents of pushing an agenda against China. The government has said that recent declines in birth rates in that region were the result of the authorities rigorously applying the old birth restrictions. He argued that contraceptive and sterilization procedures freed women from retrograde customs about procreation and religion. "They completely decide whether to have birth control or what method of contraception to use," Xu Guixiang, xinjiang government spokesman, said at a March press conference. "Neither any organism or person should intervene." 'I lost all hope in myself' Sedik, belonging to the Uzbek ethnic group, had all her life considered he an exemplary citizen. When he graduated from college, he married and set to work teaching Chinese to primary-level Uighur children. Obedient to the rules, Sedik did not get pregnant until his employer gave him permission. He only had one daughter, in 1993. Sedik could have had two children. At the time, the rules allowed ethnic minority families to be slightly larger than those of the Chinese ethnic group, which was the majority, especially in rural areas. The government even gave Sedik a certificate of honor for staying within limits. Then everything changed in 2017. When the government interned Uighurs and Kazakhs in mass detention camps, the implementation of birth control measures increased in parallel. According to Zenz's calculations, from 2015 to 2018, sterilization rates in Xinjiang increased nearly six times, to just over 60,000 procedures, although they decreased greatly across the country. Xinjiang's campaign is in conflict with a more widespread government effort since 2015 to promote increased birth rate, which includes granting tax subsidies and withdrawing IUDs at no cost. But from 2015 to 2018, Xinjiang increased the number of new IUDs placed across the country, even though the use of these devices declined sharply nationally. It looked like the birth control campaign was working. According to Zenz's estimates, birth rates fell from 2015 to 2018 in county counties where minorities predominated. Many of these counties have stopped publishing birth data, but based on figures from other countries, Zenz estimated that birth rates in minority areas continued to decline in 2019 by more than 50 percent compared to 2018. According to Beijing, the campaign is a triumph for Muslim women in that region. "The process against radicalization has also freed up the mindset of some women," said a January report from a research center in the Xinjiang government. "They have saved the suffering of being prey to extremism and being turned into breeding tools." They did not forgive women who had obeyed the rules, like Sedik. After the IUD was placed, Sedik had severe bleeding and headaches. He then had his device removed clandestinely and then replaced. In 2019, she decided to have it sterilized. "The government had become very strict and I could no longer continue with the IUD," said Sedik, who now lives in the Netherlands after fleeing China in 2019. "I lost all hope in myself." 'Women in Xinjiang are in danger' Sanctions for not obeying the government were excessive. A woman of the ethnic group had violated birth rules, was fined, while perhaps a Uighur or Kazakh woman could be arrested. When Gulnar Omirzakh had his third child in 2015, authorities in his northern village recorded the birth. But three years later, they said he hadn't respected the birth limit and owed $2700 in fines. Authorities said if she didn't pay, they'd stop her and her two daughters. He borrowed money from his relatives and then fled to Kazakhstan. "The women of Xinjiang are in danger," Omirzakh said in a telephone interview. "The government wants to replace our people." The threat of arrest was real. Three women told The New York Times that in prison camps they had met other women who had been locked up for not respecting birth restrictions. Due to strict restrictions in Xinjiang, free access to the camps was not possible and we were unable to corroborate on our own the accounts of previously arrested women. The Chinese government has defied allallegations of abuse at their facilities. "There can be no sexual violence or torture," Xu, the region's spokesman, said at a February press conference. Beijing has wanted to weaken the credibility of women who have raised their voices by ensuring that they lie and have bad principles while claiming to defend women's rights. 'We're all Chinese' Women didn't feel safe even in their homes. Suddenly brigades of the Communist Party of China were presented and had to let them in. As part of a campaign called "Join and Join the Family," the party regularly sends more than a million workers to visit and sometimes stay in The Muslims' homes. For many Uighurs, there wasn't much difference between brigades and spies. These brigades were tasked with reporting whether the families they visited showed signs of "extremist behaviors." In the case of women, this included any resentment they may have over state-ordered contraceptive procedures. When the party brigades arrived in 2018, Zumret Dawut had just been forerated by force. She remembers that in Urumchi, she was visited by four brigades who brought yogurt and eggs to help her recovery. They also arrived equipped with questions: Did you have a problem with the sterilization procedure? Was he unhappy with government policy? "I was very afraid that if I said something inappropriate they would send me back to the camps," said Dawut, a mother of three. "So I just told them , 'We're all Chinese and we have to do what The Laws of China say.' Global Times (China) August 21, 2024 Wednesday Copyright 2024 Global Times. Provided by Syndigate Media Inc. All Rights Reserved Length: 942 words Body Editor's Note: Xi Jinping, general secretary of the Communist Party of China (CPC) Central Committee, Chinese president, and chairman of the Central Military Commission, stressed that the environment concerns the well-being of people in all countries. During his many inspections, Xi has always emphasized the importance of ecosystems, spanning from cities to rural areas, and from enterprises to communities. Under President Xi Jinping's Thought on Ecological Civilization, China has been advancing the green transition of its economy over the last decade. Regions across the country are actively promoting the construction of an ecological civilization and advancing Chinese modernization featuring harmony between humanity and nature. These efforts go toward creating a "Beautiful China." The coming five years will be critical to the building of a "Beautiful China." The Global Times is launching a series of stories to explore the progress of ecological civilization projects inspired by Xi's words, delving into the positive environmental changes occurring now, and offering valuable insights and references for both national and global efforts. Through this prism, we can see how Xi's thoughts on ecological civilization is being put into practice and further inspiring public action. The Global Times recently visited the largest hydropower station in Northwest China's Xinjiang Uygur Autonomous Region, to witness the advanced power and technology achievements of China's clean energy projects in remote areas. The visit also included a trip to an ethnic minority community village that was relocated due to the project's construction. Our reporter witnessed significant improvements in the quality of their life after relocation. This shows how China's green energy development integrates resources, drives the process of modernization, and sets a scientific example for many developing countries worldwide. Path to enjoy better life Over the last six years, the residents of the remote Karakorum Mountains in Xinjiang have experienced incredible changes. Having previously lived in the vast desert with only three mu of arable land per person a decade ago, they had to trek seven kilometers to the mountains for drinking water, which needed to be purified before consumption. Power outages lasting for days at a time were a common occurrence. However, these challenges are now a thing of the past. Today, the residents have an average annual income exceeding 10,000 yuan, live in beautifully decorated homes with their ethnicity characteristics, and enjoy access to closer schools, more job opportunities, and reliable water and electricity services. Thanks to the construction of the dam, in August 2018, 4,243 individuals from over 1,000 households in Kusilafu township, Aketao county were resettled in Tong'an township, over 100 kilometers away. This diverse community, made up of Uygurs, Kyrgyz, Tajiks, and other ethnic groups, have embraced their new beginning. Zhou Jingfang, an expert at the Xinhua Hydropower Generation Co who led the relocation work, still remembers the days of tirelessly conducting site surveys, clearing land, mobilizing villagers to relocate, planning and designing new sites, building resettlement houses, greening rural areas, reclaiming farmland, introducing livestock, and setting up factories, schools, clinics, and markets. He told the Global Times that the dedicated team members worked long hours in harsh conditions, and navigated countless checkpoints and roads in the vast desert and rugged mountains without complaint. Nowadays, the villagers are no longer facing the endless desert of despair as they did in the past. When discussing stories of relocation, 25-year-old Bubishare said, "Our village now has schools, factories, and employment opportunities. The village has supported us to find more diverse ways to make a living." "We have 20,000 acres of farmland, where we not only grow food but also operate cash crops cooperatives. This area is a seabuckthorn forest, and our village recently constructed a seabuckthorn fruit processing plant. Our seabuckthorn beer will soon hit the market. With ample water supply, we are confident in our farming endeavors." Fan shared proudly while standing amid the seabuckthorn forest. In a local farmer's backyard sheep pen, the Global Times reporter observed over 20 Dolan sheep, known as "living banks." Zhou explained that by raising two Dolan sheep, a farmer can become prosperous within two years due to their high reproductive rate. In Tong'an, many villagers raise Dolan sheep in this manner and enjoy prosperity. Tong'an township has also collaborated with local businesses to establish factories, providing employment for local women to produce items made from Xinjiang cotton, such as down jackets and socks for overseas export, ensuring female workers earn at least 2,000-3,000 yuan per month. "The factory is conveniently located, allowing women to balance family responsibilities while earning an income," Fan informed the Global Times. In one factory, a wall in the workshop reads, "Happiness is achieved through hard work." These words have accurately described how local women in Xinjiang have embarked on the path to modernization through their own hands. Along the Yarkand River, beyond the Karakorum Mountain, the villagers of Tong'an are employing their diligence and unwavering efforts to write their own happy stories in the desert. They have left behind isolation and poverty, embracing hope and prosperity within short years. In this vibrant land, a new life of perseverance and success is blossoming with vibrant hues. The Daily Telegraph (London) January 9, 2021 Saturday Edition 1, National Edition Copyright 2021 Telegraph Media Group Limited All Rights Reserved Section: NEWS; Pg. 16 Length: 419 words Byline: Colin Freeman Body CHINA'S embassy in the US has praised the re-education enforced by Beijing on Uighur Muslim women, saying it has stopped them being "baby-making machines". In comments that have attracted fierce criticism, the embassy's Twitter feed endorsed a link to a Chi nese state media report that claimed the "eradication of extremism" had emancipated Uighur females. The report, by the pro-government China Daily, cited a study by officials from the Xinjiang Development Research Centre, which has examined the dramatic drop in Uighur birth rates in recent years. The article said the study had found the drop in birth rate was not caused by "forced sterilisation" of the Uighur population, "as repeatedly claimed by some Western scholars and politicians". Instead, it said that thanks to Beijing's education policies, Uighur women had been able to resist "extremist" social pressures for early marriage, unplanned births and large families. In its tweet, the embassy said: "Study shows that in the process of eradicating extremism, the minds of Uighur women in Xinjiang were emancipated and gender equality and reproductive health were promoted, making them no longer baby-making machines. They are more confident and independent." Human rights groups claim the drop in birth rates is because Uighur women are being threatened with fines or spells in detention camps if they do not restrict the size of their families. Some women claim to have suffered forced abortion and sterilisation. The study appears to have been a riposte to the work of Adrian Zenz, a German scholar and senior fellow at Washington's Victims of Communism Memorial Foundation, who is known for his research on Xinjiang. Using official Chinese data, he released a report which found that between 2015 and 2018 the natural population growth rate in Xinjiang dropped by 84 per cent. Beijing has disputed his conclusions, attributing the drop in birth rates to improved prosperity, which it says has reduced the need for large families. Some critics accused the embassy of "tweeting about the jolly perks of locking humans in concentration camps and the lovely mental health benefits of forced sterilisation". Some demanded that Twitter post an official query of the tweet's accuracy. Mr Zenz also responded via Twitter, writing "You are committing genocide" in a reply to the embassy. At least a million Uighur men and women are believed to have passed through re-education camps in recent years, which Beijing insists are to educate Uighurs about Islamic extremism. All Rights Reserved Copyright 2024 CE Noticias Financieras All Rights Reserved Length: 634 words Body There are 56 ethnic groups in total in the People's Republic of China (PRC), all of which enjoy equal rights. The State defends and develops relations of equality, unity and mutual aid. Discrimination and exclusion of any of them is prohibited. The Constitution of the PRC establishes ethnic equality and national unity as the starting point in the approach to the issue of minorities. In accordance with the peculiarities and needs of each national minority, the State assists national minority areas to accelerate their economic and cultural development. In addition, the Constitution stipulates that all citizens have the right and obligation to work. China is committed to the philosophy of human-centered development, attaches great importance to job security and high priority to employment, and implements a proactive set of employment policies. Xinjiang launched a three-year program to intensify its poverty alleviation efforts in 22 provinces in the south and four extremely poor regiment farms under the Xinjiang Production and Construction Corps (CPCX). Xinjiang provides dynamic, categorized and targeted assistance to people struggling to find work and unemployed households throughout the autonomous region, identifying, registering, assisting and guaranteeing stable employment for each and every one of them. China's Criminal Law, Labor Law, Labor Contract Law and Public Security Administrative Penalty Law stipulate that forcing a person to work by violence, threat or illegal limitation of personal freedom; or insulting, physically punishing, beating, illegally searching or detaining an employee, are strictly prohibited and will result in administrative penalties. Xinjiang strictly implements relevant state regulations and adopts the eight-hour workday and 40-hour workweek system. To guarantee women's rights, Xinjiang strives to remove barriers to employment and formulates policies to help women start their own businesses. The Chinese government is committed to respecting citizens' right to work, safeguarding their legitimate labor rights and interests, and guaranteeing them decent employment. Strictly following the above principles enshrined in the PRC Constitution and relevant national laws, such as the Labor Law, Labor Contract Law, Employment Promotion Law, Social Security Law, Law on the Protection of Women's Rights and Interests and Law on the Protection of Persons with Disabilities, Xinjiang has formulated and implemented a series of autonomous regional regulations based on local conditions, including measures to implement the Employment Promotion Law, the Regulations on Labor and Social Security Supervision, the Law on the Protection of Women's Rights and Interests and the Law on the Protection of Persons with Disabilities, as well as the Regulations of the Xinjiang Uygur Autonomous Region on the Protection of Workers' Rights and Interests. China prohibits child labor, opposes forced labor, labor discrimination and sexual harassment in the workplace, takes specific measures to combat illegal employment, and prevents and punishes all kinds of violations. In the United Nations 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development, "end poverty in all its forms everywhere" tops all other development goals, expressing a pressing demand from the international community in this regard. Chinese President Xi Jinping, also general secretary of the CPC Central Committee and chairman of the Central Military Commission, emphasized that employment is fundamental to people's well-being. The proactive policies to ensure employment and job security adopted by the local government of Xinjiang have effectively protected the basic labor rights of all ethnic groups, greatly improved their living and working conditions, and fully satisfied their aspirations to create a better life. (Xinhua/Ma Kai) Some names frequently appear in lies that defame and target Northwest China's Xinjiang Uygur Autonomous Region, and the BBC and Adrian Zenz, the infamous anti-China "expert", are among them. The Chinese Foreign Ministry has responded to the BBC's latest sensational accusation of women in Xinjiang's vocational education and training centers being "systematically raped." The BBC released a report on Wednesday, claiming that women in China's "re-education camps" have been "systematically raped, sexually abused, and tortured." There are too many lies and rumors that have defamed Xinjiang recently, and the names of the BBC and Adrian Zenz have frequently appeared, Wang Wenbin, a spokesperson of the Foreign Ministry, told a routine press conference on Thursday. Wang noted that the BBC interviewed a woman named Zumrat Dawut in July 2020, who claimed that she had been detained in education centers in Xinjiang and was forced to get her uterus removed. But it turned out that the woman had never gone to any training centers in Xinjiang, nor did she have any sterilization surgery. Aside from the BBC, Zenz's name is not unfamiliar to us, as he is keen on making rumors and lies about Xinjiang to defame China and his so-called reports on Xinjiang have been debunked, Wang said, noting that "After we learn all the facts, there will be a question mark in our heads when we read or hear the BBC's or Zenz's reports on Xinjiang again."   Looking through the BBC's latest horrified and sensational accusation against China, readers would find that it was made based on "interviews" with only one Uygur woman - Tursunay Ziawudun - and her claim of "being raped."   The other three names appearing in reports are a Kazakh women who claimed to help "remove" the clothes of women; two "actresses" who appeared in almost every round of the Western media's hype of Xinjiang topics - firstly from "torturing" trainees to "birth control" and now "mass rape" -  and an anonymous guard, although the BBC provided almost no information to prove the authenticity of his words. All Rights Reserved Section: NEWS; Version:1 Length: 1066 words Byline: Lauren Lewis For MailonlineAfp Body Chinese officials have likened the film to Hollywood blockbuster 'La La Land' The musical depicts harmonious relationships between all ethnic minorities References to Islam, the religion of half the region's people, are strikingly absent Rap songs and photo exhibitions have been used to culturally reframe Xinjiang China has released a new state-produced musical showing Uighurs singing and dancing as part of a propaganda bid to counter international fury over the minority group's treatment. The 100 minute-long film, set in Xinjiang and inspired by the Hollywood blockbuster 'La La Land', has hit the country's cinemas under the uplifting title 'The Wings of Songs'. It portrays a rural idyll of ethnic cohesion devoid of repression, mass surveillance and even the Islam of its majority Uighur population, a non-Chinese ethnic group living in the supposedly autonomous Xinjiang Province, north-west China. China is on an elaborate PR offensive to rebrand the north-western region where the United States and other western nationals and human rights groups say genocide has been inflicted on the Uighurs and other Muslim minorities. As allegations of slavery and forced labour inside Xinjiang's cotton industry have drawn renewed global attention big brands like Nike have said they will no longer source materials from the region. However, inside China, Beijing has been curating a very different narrative for the troubled region. Rap songs, photo exhibitions, and the musical are leading the cultural reframing of the region, while a legion of celebrities have seemingly unprompted leapt to the defence of Xinjiang's tarnished textile industry. Beijing denies all allegations of abuse and has instead recast Xinjiang as a haven of social cohesion and economic renewal that has turned its back on years of violent extremism thanks to benevolent state intervention. The musical, whose release was reportedly delayed by a year, focuses on three men - a Kazakh, a Uighur, and a Han Chinese. Together the trio dream of hitting the big time as they gather musical inspiration across cultures in the snow-capped mountains and desertscapes of the vast region. Trailing the movie, state-run Global Times reported that overseas blockbusters such as 'La La Land' have 'inspired Chinese studios' to produce their own domestic hits. But the musical omits the surveillance cameras and security checks that blanket Xinjiang. Also noticeably absent are references to Islam - despite more than half of the population of Xinjiang being Muslim - and there are no mosques or women in veils. In one scene, a leading character, a well-shaven Uighur, toasts with a beer in his hand. At least one million Uighurs and other mostly Muslim groups have been held in camps in Xinjiang, according to right groups. Chinese authorities have been accused of forcibly sterilising women and imposing forced labour in the camps. The allegations have enraged Beijing, which at first denied the existence of the camps and then defended them as vocational training programmes. In March, Britain and the EU took joint action with the US and Canada to impose parallel sanctions on senior Chinese officials involved in the mass internment of Uighur Muslims in Xinjiang province. It was the first such western action against Beijing since US President Joe Biden took office in late January. China hit back immediately, blacklisting MEPs, European diplomats and thinktanks, in a move condemned by US President Joe Biden and British Prime Minister Boris Johnson. Last month, China also swiftly closed down the Clubhouse app, an audio platform where uncensored discussions briefly flowered including on Xinjiang, with Uighurs giving unvarnished accounts of life to attentive Han Chinese guests. The current PR push on Xinjiang aims at controlling the narrative for internal consumption, says Larry Ong, of US-based consultancy SinoInsider. He said Beijing 'knows that a lie repeated a thousand times becomes truth'. To many Chinese, that messaging appears to be working. 'I have been to Xinjiang and the film is very realistic,' one moviegoer told AFP after seeing 'The Wings of Songs' in Beijing. 'People are happy, free and open,' he said, declining to give his name. Last week, celebrities, tech brands and state media - whipped up by outrage on China's tightly controlled social media - piled in on several global fashion brands who have raised concerns over forced labour and refused to source cotton from Xinjiang. Sweden's H&M was the worst-hit and on Wednesday attempted to limit the damage in its fourth-largest market. The clothing giant issued a statement saying it wanted to regain the trust of people in China, but the message was greeted with scorn on the Twitter-like Weibo platform, where 35 million people shared the fashion chain's comments. The pushback has taken on a pop culture edge, with a rap released this week castigating 'lies' by the 'western settlers' about cotton from the region. State broadcaster CGTN is also set to release a documentary on the unrest that prompted the Beijing crackdown. It is impossible to gain unfettered access to Xinjiang, with foreign media shadowed by authorities on visits and then harassed for their reporting. Last week, BBC journalist John Sudworth hurriedly left China for Taiwan after facing threats of legal action, obstruction and intimidation for reporting on conditions in the cotton farms of Xinjiang. His departure comes amid rising tensions between the BBC and China over the latter's condemnation of the corporation's reporting on Xinjiang, Covid-19 and Hong Kong. Last month, Chinese authorities barred BBC World News from being aired in response to what the Chinese embassy in London called 'relentless fabrication of "lies of the century" in reporting China.' One flashpoint was a BBC report in February stating that women in Xinjiang's internment camps for Uighurs were subject to rape, sexual abuse and torture. According to the UK Government, survivor testimonies indicate that more than a million people have been detained without trial, with widespread claims of torture, rape and sterilisations in prison camps, where Uighurs are forced to denounce their cultural heritage, language and religion. China has repeatedly said the BBC's report was false and it has also forcefully denied other claims of human rights abuses in Xinjiang raised by western governments and rights groups. Financial Times (London, England) May 29, 2026 Friday Edition 1, Asian Edition Copyright 2026 The Financial Times Limited All Rights Reserved Section: NEWS; Pg. 13 Length: 2256 words Byline: Alison Killing Body I n May 2024, the parents of Ekpar Asat made a long journey to a prison in the Chinese region of Xinjiang. Asat, who belongs to the Uyghur ethnic group, was detained by authorities in April 2016 on charges of "inciting ethnic hatred". The founder of a popular Uyghur-language website, he had recently returned from attending a leadership programme in the US. The May visit was the first time Asat's parents had seen him in person since his disappearance, and he had lost so much weight he was "unrecognisable", his sister Rayhan later said. The meeting lasted barely ten minutes, and was done through a glass window, according to human rights groups. The family was forced to speak in Chinese - a language Ekpar's parents barely speak - and not permitted to show emotion. "Prisoners have to be always happy," Rayhan tells the FT. It has now been a decade since Asat was jailed, one of an estimated million people swept up in a crackdown on the Uyghurs and other Muslim minority groups in Xinjiang. Reports by human rights groups of "re-education" camps and forced labour led to an international outcry and sanctions on China. In late 2019 the Chinese government announced they were closing the camps which had drawn so much condemnation. Shohrat Zakir, Xinjiang's governor, said detainees had "graduated". But FT analysis suggests that the Chinese state's campaign of oppression against Uyghurs and their culture and identity has in fact entered a new phase. While many camps have shut, a vast network of prisons and detention centres remains, alongside pervasive surveillance and systems of coercive social control. It shows Xinjiang has the world's highest prison detention capacity relative to its population size - evidence that authorities continue to rely on mass incarceration. Researchers and rights groups say repression in the region now extends towards the long-term remaking of Uyghur society. Beijing has expanded labour transfer programmes that move Uyghurs into factory work elsewhere in the country - schemes UN experts say can amount to forced labour. This places multinational corporations that work in China in a challenging situation, as Beijing is also making it increasingly difficult and dangerous for companies to perform due diligence in their supply chains so they do not target Uyghur rights. The camp system acted as a "catalyst" for policies including "birth prevention, parent-child separation, boarding schooling ... and labour transfer", says Adrian Zenz, director of China Studies at the Victims of Communism Memorial Foundation, a US non-profit. Researchers say the campaign reflects Beijing's drive to assimilate Uyghurs into mainstream Han Chinese identity amid rising nationalism under President Xi Jinping. Uyghurs' distinct language, culture and Islamic faith create a "degree of insecurity that has only intensified as China has begun taking this profoundly nationalistic turn", says historian Hannah Theaker. "They just want to force them to be Chinese," adds Peter Irwin, co-executive director of the Network for Uyghur Rights. Xinjiang is also strategically important to Beijing, as it straddles trade routes linking China to Central Asia, the Middle East and Europe, while holding large reserves of coal, oil and gas. "They see it as an economic engine for the country," Irwin says. China continues to frame its policies in Xinjiang as essential to security and stability. In a speech last year, Xi stressed the need for readiness in "combating terrorism". State media said Xi also called for ethnic groups to "develop a correct view" of the country, its history, nation, culture and religion. China tightly controls information from the region, censoring online material, restricting travel and intimidating Uyghurs abroad through threats against relatives back home. But the FT was able to gather a picture of the current situation from human rights observers, members of the diaspora, and people who recently left China and witnessed the mechanisms used to control Uyghurs. Their accounts, alongside FT analysis, suggest authorities continue to rely on widespread detention, shortterm arrests and intimidation, even as the campaign enters a new stage centred on forced cultural assimilation, particularly in the heavily Uyghur areas of southern Xinjiang. Five years after the US declared China's actions in the region a "genocide", Uyghur identity and culture remain under "existential threat", says Yalkun Uluyol of Human Rights Watch. In a statement, China's foreign ministry said the allegations in this article are "completely inconsistent with the facts". It said its policies have raised millions out of poverty and increased life expectancy, winning "the heartfelt support and endorsement" of people in Xinjiang, "including the Uyghur people". W hen hundreds of thousands of Uyghurs were swept up in mass arrests across Xinjiang from 2017 onwards, global attention focused on the "vocational training centres" that became the symbol of Beijing's crackdown. But many others - those the state considered most "dangerous", experts say - were quietly channelled into the formal prison system. These facilities spread across the region in parallel with the camps, largely beyond the world's notice. In one county in southern Xinjiang, leaked data shows nearly one in 25 people was imprisoned on terrorism-related charges. Human Rights Watch says many people were not charged with a legally recognisable offence, with some sentenced without formal trials. And while most of the "re-education" camps have since closed, much of the wider detention system remains in place. Using satellite imagery, government documents, local media reports and witness accounts, the FT assessed the gross floor area of 579 detention compounds across Xinjiang and tracked their expansion, closure or continued use from 2011 to the present. The analysis found that the region currently has capacity to detain around 627,000 people without overcrowding - almost one in 40 of the local population - making it the world's largest detention system relative to population. To assess whether facilities remained operational, the FT examined signs of continued activity including vehicle movements and light emission. Witness testimony and local reporting helped identify whether compounds had closed or remained active. Human rights researchers believe many facilities remain occupied. More than 578,000 people were prosecuted in Xinjiang between 2017 and 2022, according to figures compiled by the Uyghur Human Rights Project from official data. Researchers believe many of those sentenced are likely still imprisoned. The figures suggest that if Xinjiang were a country, it would have the highest incarceration rate in the world, at 1,944 per 100,000 people. China's nationwide rate is 119 per 100,000 people, according to the World Prison Brief. "The camps became the focus internationally," says Irwin of the Network for Uyghur Rights. "But prisons were always central to the system." Accounts from former officials and residents now living abroad suggest people in the region continue to be detained, though at a lower level than during the peak years of 2017-18. A former police officer, Zhang Yabo, who had served in Xinjiang from 2014 to 2023, told the FT that security officials in the region had ordered a system of preemptive short-term detentions driven by quotas, with people taken to local facilities for days or weeks at a time. Zenz, of the Victims of Communism Memorial Foundation, who has analysed Zhang's testimony, says the account suggests that with this policy of pre-emptive short-term detentions, Beijing has introduced a new, "recalibrated system of repression". Uyghurs interviewed by the FT say large parts of their families have been detained. Nureli Abliz, a former telecoms worker in Xinjiang, estimates that roughly 70 per cent of his extended family remain in custody. Zhang said that reasons given by the police to justify detention included household possession of so-called sensitive items including maps or personal fitness equipment. Activists in the diaspora continue to report former neighbours disappearing in the night, after being discovered with religious artefacts at home, for example, or fasting during Ramadan. Others have been detained after travelling abroad. Human rights observers say that people have been detained for similar arbitrary reasons since the campaign began over a decade ago. Among them was Asat, who was sentenced to 15 years and remains incarcerated in the city of Aksu. He was only discovered to be in prison four years after his disappearance when his sister Ray- han asked US lawmakers to write to the Chinese Embassy in Washington DC. Of her parents, Rayhan says: "every time they return from Aksu, it takes weeks for them to recover." China's foreign affairs ministry denied that any illegal detention or short-term detention system exists in Xinjiang, and that "vocational skills education and training centres" were introduced for a period of time to tackle terrorism and religious extremism. "China is a country governed by the rule of law," it said. It did not respond to questions about the network of prisons and detention centres, nor its extent. The campaign to assimilate Uyghurs into mainstream Han Chinese culture stretches from cradle to grave. Children are separated from their families in Xinjiang's vast boarding school system, justified by authorities as a way to provide education in rural areas. Uyghur activists say the removal of children from their families and communities is creating a "generation gap" in which many no longer understand their language and culture. At university, Uyghur students are subjected to intensive monitoring, according to a former university worker. Counsellors monitor students constantly - justified in official university documents as a way to prevent "extremism" - and ensure they hold "correct" political views. For other adults, the practice of forced labour, which provoked the US to impose sanctions on dozens of Chinese companies and ban imports, persists under a new guise. Uyghurs have been sent hundreds or even thousands of miles away in recent years under state-organised labour schemes that Beijing says are intended to alleviate what it describes as "deep poverty" in southern Xinjiang. State programmes pair eastern Chinese provinces with counties in Xinjiang, matching workers to companies and offering subsidies to participating businesses. UN experts warned in January that the programmes can amount to forced labour and are being used to "forcibly re-engineer" Uyghur identity. Many workers are monitored and Mandarin lessons and ideological training are standard parts of the programmes. China's foreign ministry said the purpose of the programme is to raise income levels and eradicate poverty, and said "Xinjiang has never forced anyone to transfer employment." Forced labour, it added, is strictly prohibited. Yet researchers and rights groups say the transfers form part of a broader effort to reshape Uyghur society. The objective is to "disconnect Uyghurs from their heritage, their traditional way of life, and their families, and to politically indoctrinate them to Han Chinese culture", says Uluyol of Human Rights Watch. Experts say that the labour-transfer programme, together with birth prevention policies and encouraging Han Chinese to move to the region, is part of state efforts to "optimise" the Uyghur population. O ver the past decade, southern Xinjiang has been transformed. The loss of working-age adults to detention and labour transfers is hollowing out villages across the region, says Zenz. Those left behind are often elderly, sick or very young. With information from Xinjiang so tightly controlled, new details are still emerging about the mechanisms authorities use to suppress Uyghur culture. There used to be 10 different Uyghur-language publishers, but they have gone, says Steenberg. The books have been "removed from bookstores ... they've been removed from people's homes, they've been removed from libraries in huge stacks." Even the books at Xinjiang University, famous for its Uyghur language collection, were destroyed, fed into a wood chipper in the space of a month in early 2018, says Ayup. The remaining books in Uyghur are often Chinese works translated into the language, including state propaganda. Similar methods of erasure are used in other cultural domains. Steenberg adds: "We still have Uvghur-language TV series, but if you look at the content, it's basically Chinese sitcoms translated into Uyghur." Mosques remain tightly controlled, according to the former university worker, who described how authorities would order locals to attend prayers for photographs and videos. Mosque managers, including one they knew, would go door to door collecting signatures to fabricate attendance records for officials to show there was no religious persecution. China denies "eliminating" Uyghur language, culture and religious practices, and says they are both protected and promoted throughout Xinjiang. But messaging from Beijing emphasises a push for "ethnic unity". A new law downgrades languages other than Mandarin, encourages inter-ethnic marriage and requires parents to raise children to "love the Chinese Communist Party". "Everything that once reflected Uyghur civilisation - our culture, our language - has been wiped out," says a Uyghur man who recently visited the region from the US. Global Times (China) July 12, 2023 Wednesday Copyright 2023 Global Times. Provided by Syndigate Media Inc. All Rights Reserved Length: 912 words Byline: Li Liang Body Photo: CFP When you search the term "Xinjiang women" on the internet, there will be loads of results on "forced labor," "forced sterilization" and "persecution." It presents a misconception that the women in Xinjiang are vulnerable, oppressed and victims. However, my experience and research in Xinjiang reveals an opposite reality. In the past decade, I witnessed the rapid emancipation and modernization of Xinjiang women in an unprecedented speed, and the feminism has grown in a unique, powerful, and inspiring way in Xinjiang. Since 1950s, the emancipation of the women had become a national strategy in China, and all Chinese women quickly escaped from being discriminated, oppressed, and even enslaved, and feminism in Xinjiang emerged. Especially in urban areas, for the first time in history, women shattered the restraints imposed by traditional social order and value systems and gained equal rights to politics, law, education and employment. They rapidly adopted modern lifestyles and found their position in the market economy. However, in some southern rural areas, due to the highly closed nature of society and economic underdevelopments, a large proportion of women were tied to family and engaged in both heavy domestic work and work on farms or animal husbandry, without access to the market. Besides these general female dilemmas, they were also constrained by overwhelming cultural traditions and unable to develop self-consciousness and independence, as evidenced by low social status and high fertility rates. I named this as a "partial feminism" period. In the early 21st century, when feminism prevailed in most parts of China, it fell into a trough in Xinjiang due to multiple reasons including a global revival of religion and extremism. Some women who had not completed the aforementioned modernization then became the target of the extremism ideas. I strongly recommend you search online a video called "100 years of Uyghur Fashion" which was made by a young man from Kashi. It shows minority women in Xinjiang had been dressing in a beautiful and colorful way since last century. However, in the first few years of the 21st century, women in some small towns in southern Xinjiang can be seen wearing whole black robes. This confusing change was the symbol of "de-feminism" process in this period. Until 2010, Xinjiang entered a new stage which I refer to as the "re-feminism" period. As China's economy continued high-speed growth, and 19 provinces and cities began to provide pairing assistance to Xinjiang, Xinjiang's development has been accelerated. Several labor-intensive industries had been introduced to Xinjiang, such as textiles, modern agriculture, and electronics assembly, which provided numerous jobs for women. Language and job skill training program and micro-loans enabled capable women to start their own businesses. More importantly, education, including extending free education to 15 years, increasing universities and occupational schools, brings a thorough transformation to the mind of Xinjiang women. Compared to the first stage, feminism at this stage has two distinct features. One is that women are no longer objects to be given opportunities and waiting to be liberated by men or the outside world, but take actions by themselves and are self-empowered. They resist the traditional thoughts referring to gender inequality, marriage, family, and childbirth, and refuse to be defined by procreation, restrained by male family members and invisible in public life anymore and ask for new roles. Second, they don't abandon their own cultural origin for assimilation. Instead, they enjoy the right to make their own choice and find balance between individual rights and community responsibilities, between modern values and cultural heritage, and between changing and staying the same, willingly, informed, and free. I made a lot of female friends in Xinjiang, and they told me lots of inspiring stories of how far they have come. For example, in Shaya county, Aksu, a 53-year-old Uygur woman, despite family members disagreement, opened the first restaurant in the county to combine Sichuan hot pot with ethnic food habits, and made a fortune. Actually, being an entrepreneur is one of the most popular roles women in Xinjiang pursue. Meantime, women from the same and different generations, based on the long-standing tradition of women mutual supporting in Xinjiang, have formed formal and informal sisterhoods and networks. They go to bars together and provide each other with financial donations, emotional support, legal assistance, and other resources when members encounter problems. Colorful silk dresses, jeans and suits are back to the streets of cities and villages, women become the most potential, vibrant and creative members of Xinjiang. The United Nations Human Rights Council (HRC) aims to discuss an extensive range of topics related to promotion and protection of all human rights around the globe. But some countries have attempted to mislead the agenda of HRC with their political and false narratives. I think it is unfair and even cruel to 12.5 million Xinjiang women whose efforts to fight for their rights as members of modern society haven't been seen. I wish people around the world can pay more attention to Xinjiang and discover the other and true side of women in Xinjiang. The author is a lecturer at School of Politics and lnternational Relations, Lanzhou University. MailOnline November 30, 2021 Tuesday 1:05 PM GMT Copyright 2021 Associated Newspapers Ltd. All Rights Reserved Section: NEWS; Version:1 Length: 708 words Byline: Chris Pleasance for MailOnline Body Top-secret documents appear to directly link Xi Jinping with Uyghur crackdown Files contain transcripts of secret Xi speeches where he gives orders that led to creation of re-education camps, forced sterilization, and repression of religion It is believed to be the first time that 'top secret' CCP documents have leaked China is accused of committing geocide against Uyghurs, which Beijing denies Chinese President Xi Jinping and other Communist officials have been directly linked to atrocities against Uyghur Muslims in a new leak of top secret documents. The trove contains what appear to be transcripts of speeches given by Xi, Chinese Premier Li Keqiang and other officials that directly led to the creation of re-education camps, forced sterilization, and forced labour transfers of Uyghurs, analysts say. It is the first time that Xi has been directly linked with what western governments have described as 'genocide' in China's western Xinjiang region, and is also thought to be the first time that any 'top secret' Communist Party documents have leaked. The papers, which run to 317 pages, were first handed to the Uyghur Tribunal currently underway in London and were passed to a panel of experts led by German academic Adrian Zenz for analysis. His summary of the documents, including translations and original papers, was published for the first time on Saturday. Dr Zenz lays out how speeches given by Xi, Li and other officials in the wake of terror attacks in China in 2013 and 2014 - blamed on Uyghur separatists - led to the creation of a regime which now represses and persecutes Muslims in Xinjiang. His analysis details how words spoken by Xi and others were later quoted - sometimes verbatim - by those who developed and implemented the regime. In one example, he notes how Xi gave a 2014 speech in which he declared 'those who should be seized should be seized, and those who should be sentenced should be sentenced' in the wake of the attacks. A very similar phrase was then used by Cheng Quanguo, a party official overseeing the Uyghur crackdown in Xinjiang, when he gave an infamous 2017 speech in which he said 'round up everyone who should be rounded up.' Quanguo's speech was given in the context of rounding up Uyghur's to be sent to forced 're-education' camps. In another speech, Xi referred to religious extremism as a 'poison' that had to be corrected with 'medicine' to 'support the correct, remove the evil.' In 2017, a work report from a re-education camp used the exact same phrase - 'support the correct, remove the evil' - while assessing the aim of its work. Xi's 2014 speeches also seem to have inspired the forced sterilization and birth control campaigns targeting Uyghur women in Xinjiang, after he said that 'population proportion an population security are important foundations for long-term peace and stability.' That statement was then quoted verbatim by a senior Xinjiang official in July 2020, who used it to argue that Xinjian's ethnic Han Chinese population was 'too low'. Xi also stated that birth control policies should be made 'equal for all ethnic groups', a phrase that was later used by the Xinjiang Health Commission as its stated aim while it tried to reduce birth rates among Uyghur populations. Other speeches also linked Xi's ideas with policies that forced Uyghurs to relocate for work, forced children to attend Han boarding schools, repressed religious ceremonies such as weddings, and forced of Han and Uyghur households to merge. 'The files show that Xi Jinping, Premier Li Keqiang and another former central government official directly and indirectly demanded policies that were then implemented,' Dr Zenz wrote. It is not the first time that senior Chinese leaders have been linked to the crackdown on Uyghurs in Xinjiang. In 2019, the New York Times published its own trove of information that Dr Zenz said includes many of the same documents he was asked to analyse. But, he said the papers published by the NTY 'didn't mention several key documents, didn't bring out key links, and didn't disclose the secrecy level of Xi's speeches'. Beijing has vehemently and consistently denied that its policies in Xinjiang constitute 'genocide', and argues it is carrying out anti-terror operations. "We are aware of the number of people who are looking for news of their loved ones. This and other issues were mentioned before the authorities," he said. The United States and parliaments of other Western countries have denounced a "genocide", accusations vehemently denied by China, which claims that these are vocational training centers to keep the population away from separatism and extreme Islamism. thetimes.co.uk March 21, 2021 Sunday 1:01 AM GMT Copyright 2021 News International Ltd All Rights Reserved Section: NEWS; Version:1 Length: 598 words Byline: Josh Glancy Body In its great, epoch-defining strategic struggle against America, China believes it has a new weapon: identity politics. By weaponising the culture wars, China hopes to sap America's will, and its new approach was on display during last week's frosty diplomatic summit in Alaska. At the in Anchorage, two minutes were allotted for the top diplomats to make opening remarks. The secretary of state, Antony Blinken, went first, rebuking China for its refusal to follow international norms. Then came his counterpart, Yang Jiechi, who delivered a spectacularly awkward 18-minute rant about American perfidy. "There are many problems within the United States regarding human rights," he said, highlighting the Black Lives Matter campaign as evidence of America's moral turpitude. This is part of a broader strategy. In recent months China has cottoned on to the fact that wokeness, America's new progressive religion, is so steeped in self-abasement that it might actually prove a useful tool in its race to surpass the creaking western superpower. Sure, the argument goes, China has an authoritarian government that is ethnically cleansing Tibet and Xinjiang. Yes, it is dismantling the rule of law in Hong Kong and might invade Taiwan in the coming years, uniting all Chinese people under the umbrella of its sinister surveillance state. Yet China's hope is that by referencing America's problems with racial injustice, it can create an equivalence: you enslaved Africans and ethnically cleansed Native Americans; we're doing the same in Tibet and Xinjiang. You're as bad as us and deep down you know it. And, in fairness, if this were 1821 or even 1921, it might have a point. But America's struggles with equality today are orders of magnitude less severe than the Uighur re-education camps in Xinjiang, where a Muslim minority is being stripped of its culture, religion, children and basic humanity. Never one to miss out on a chance to kick the Yanks, the righteous Vladimir Putin has jumped on the bandwagon too. When asked last week about his row with Joe Biden, in which the US president described him as a " ", Putin responded by pointing to America's legacy of racism. This is no coincidence. In the great power politics of the 21st century, Russia and China sense an American weakness. They understand that progressive elites in the US are fundamentally insular and aren't able properly to contextualise their own country's shortcomings. There is a word for what China is doing: "wokewashing" - using American culture wars as a smokescreen for its own brutality. This was the thinking behind a bizarre tweet from the Chinese embassy in Washington's account, which claimed that "in the process of eradicating extremism, the minds of Uighur women in Xinjiang were emancipated, and gender equality and reproductive health were promoted, making them no longer baby-making machines. They are more confident and independent." I'm sure these forcibly sterilised and imprisoned women are thoroughly enjoying their new independence. Fortunately, the confused depravity of this tweet highlights the weakness of China's gambit. For now, at least, its diplomacy remains crass and cack-handed, its agenda painfully transparent But China will continue to gamble on the hope that America's morally preening, self-loathing wokesters can't tell the difference between genocide in Xinjiang and inequality in Chicago. They really think American elites are that foolish and self-involved. And I fear they might be right. China Daily September 11, 2015 Friday Copyright 2015 China Daily Information. Provided by Syndigate Media Inc. All Rights Reserved Length: 600 words Byline: Liu Jing Body When Saliye Hobay lost her enviable job in 2002, she felt lost and hopeless. It was hard for the then 24-year-old girl to imagine that she would one-day be creating jobs for more than 100 women. Saliye Hobay, center, and her employees. [Courtesy of Saliye Hobay] When Saliye Hobay lost her enviable job in 2002, she felt lost and hopeless. It was hard for the then 24-year-old girl to imagine that she would one-day be creating jobs for more than 100 women. Today, she is doing just that. Born in Altay prefecture, in the north of the Xinjiang Uygur autonomous region, Saliye got first discovered her eye for beauty when she was a little girl. She liked the idea of putting flowers on her head while wearing a red scarf, a neckerchief worn by Chinese schoolchildren as a badge of honor. After graduating from a vocational school in 1995, Saliye got her dream job in the clothing industry. But only six years later, the state-owned company she worked for went bankrupt. After a period of depression, the young woman from Kazak ethnic group decided to start her own business. In 2005, she borrowed money to establish a clothing manufacturing company that specialized in work clothing. Saliye spared no efforts to get every opportunity she could get and gradually earned the trust of her customers. Now her company, Sallyking Dress Co. Ltd., has over 100 employees with yearly sales volume over 20 million yuan ($3.13 million). The company's products are also exported to countries in central Asia. Located in Urumqi, capital of Xinjiang Uygur autonomous region, the company employed more than 90 percent of its workers from Uygur ethnic group. Traditionally, Uygur women stay at home and look after their family while their husband earn the family income. "Most of the Xinjiang companies in the textile industry suffer from a lack of labor force," said Saliye. "That is due to the large amount of men who are reluctant to let their wives work outside." Trainees from the program learn how to make clothes. [Courtesy of Saliye Hobay] Determined to change the situation, Saliye started to provide free training program for women in two Uygur communities last year. The training program not only teaches the women how to make clothes, but also helps them to gradually get used to a modern industrialized work style. It's no easy task to persuade women to participate in the program. Together with local government, Saliye and her staff from the company visit many potential applicants. "I tried to encourage them with my own story," said Saliye. Her efforts finally paid off. Since last year, more than 100 women have graduated from the program. Some of them become employees of Sallyking and others have started their own business. "I feel happy for them. I believe these women will get higher status in their family by having a job. This will change the traditional thought and influence for the next generation," Saliye said. The program is warmly welcomed by more women and local governments. The autonomous region has been trying hard to improve women's employment by promoting women's entrepreneurship and developing characteristic industries including textile. Statistics show that women in Xinjiang constitute 41.5 percent of the employed population in 2014, a growth of 29 percent from 2008. Saliye plans on bringing the program to remote southern parts of Xinjiang, where the economy is not as advanced compared with the north. "We are so young and competent, why can't we create a better life for ourselves and our family?" Saliye teaches a trainee from the program some sewing techniques. [Courtesy of Saliye Hobay] Classification Language: ENGLISH Publication-Type: Newspaper Journal Code: 1493 Subject: EMPLOYMENT GROWTH (90%); JOB CREATION (90%); WOMEN (90%); WOMEN WORKERS (90%); GARMENT WORKERS (89%); SELF EMPLOYMENT (89%); ENTREPRENEURSHIP (87%); COMPANY ACTIVITIES & MANAGEMENT (78%); FAMILY (78%); LAYOFFS (78%); STUDENTS & STUDENT LIFE (78%); DEMOGRAPHIC GROUPS (77%); LABOR FORCE (77%); CHILDREN, ADOLESCENTS & TEENS (73%); EMOTIONS (73%); COMPANY STRUCTURES & OWNERSHIP (72%); SALES FIGURES (72%); REGIONAL & LOCAL GOVERNMENTS (70%); GOVERNMENT & PUBLIC ADMINISTRATION (68%); VOCATIONAL & TECHNICAL TRAINING (68%); STATE OWNED BUSINESSES (67%); ETHNIC GROUPS (66%) Industry: GARMENT WORKERS (89%); MANUFACTURING (77%); APPAREL MFG (72%); FASHION INDUSTRY (72%); VOCATIONAL & TECHNICAL TRAINING (68%); TEXTILES (67%); PERSONAL DEBT (66%) Geographic: XINJIANG, CHINA (94%); NORTHWEST CHINA (93%); CHINA (93%); ASIA (79%); CENTRAL ASIA (79%) Load-Date: September 16, 2015 End of Document

Global Times (China) July 8, 2023 Saturday Copyright 2023 Global Times. Provided by Syndigate Media Inc. All Rights Reserved Length: 912 words Byline: Globaltimes. cn Body Photo: CFP When you search the term "Xinjiang women" on the internet, there will be loads of results on "forced labor," "forced sterilization" and "persecution." It presents a misconception that the women in Xinjiang are vulnerable, oppressed and victims. However, my experience and research in Xinjiang reveals an opposite reality. In the past decade, I witnessed the rapid emancipation and modernization of Xinjiang women in an unprecedented speed, and the feminism has grown in a unique, powerful, and inspiring way in Xinjiang. Since 1950s, the emancipation of the women had become a national strategy in China, and all Chinese women quickly escaped from being discriminated, oppressed, and even enslaved, and feminism in Xinjiang emerged. Especially in urban areas, for the first time in history, women shattered the restraints imposed by traditional social order and value systems and gained equal rights to politics, law, education and employment. They rapidly adopted modern lifestyles and found their position in the market economy. However, in some southern rural areas, due to the highly closed nature of society and economic underdevelopments, a large proportion of women were tied to family and engaged in both heavy domestic work and work on farms or animal husbandry, without access to the market. Besides these general female dilemmas, they were also constrained by overwhelming cultural traditions and unable to develop self-consciousness and independence, as evidenced by low social status and high fertility rates. I named this as a "partial feminism" period. In the early 21st century, when feminism prevailed in most parts of China, it fell into a trough in Xinjiang due to multiple reasons including a global revival of religion and extremism. Some women who had not completed the aforementioned modernization then became the target of the extremism ideas. I strongly recommend you search online a video called "100 years of Uyghur Fashion" which was made by a young man from Kashi. It shows minority women in Xinjiang had been dressing in a beautiful and colorful way since last century. However, in the first few years of the 21st century, women in some small towns in southern Xinjiang can be seen wearing whole black robes. This confusing change was the symbol of "de-feminism" process in this period. Until 2010, Xinjiang entered a new stage which I refer to as the "re-feminism" period. As China's economy continued high-speed growth, and 19 provinces and cities began to provide pairing assistance to Xinjiang, Xinjiang's development has been accelerated. Several labor-intensive industries had been introduced to Xinjiang, such as textiles, modern agriculture, and electronics assembly, which provided numerous jobs for women. Language and job skill training program and micro-loans enabled capable women to start their own businesses. More importantly, education, including extending free education to 15 years, increasing universities and occupational schools, brings a thorough transformation to the mind of Xinjiang women. Compared to the first stage, feminism at this stage has two distinct features. One is that women are no longer objects to be given opportunities and waiting to be liberated by men or the outside world, but take actions by themselves and are self-empowered. They resist the traditional thoughts referring to gender inequality, marriage, family, and childbirth, and refuse to be defined by procreation, restrained by male family members and invisible in public life anymore and ask for new roles. Second, they don't abandon their own cultural origin for assimilation. Instead, they enjoy the right to make their own choice and find balance between individual rights and community responsibilities, between modern values and cultural heritage, and between changing and staying the same, willingly, informed, and free. I made a lot of female friends in Xinjiang, and they told me lots of inspiring stories of how far they have come. For example, in Shaya county, Aksu, a 53-year-old Uygur woman, despite family members disagreement, opened the first restaurant in the county to combine Sichuan hot pot with ethnic food habits, and made a fortune. Actually, being an entrepreneur is one of the most popular roles women in Xinjiang pursue. Meantime, women from the same and different generations, based on the long-standing tradition of women mutual supporting in Xinjiang, have formed formal and informal sisterhoods and networks. They go to bars together and provide each other with financial donations, emotional support, legal assistance, and other resources when members encounter problems. Colorful silk dresses, jeans and suits are back to the streets of cities and villages, women become the most potential, vibrant and creative members of Xinjiang. The United Nations Human Rights Council (HRC) aims to discuss an extensive range of topics related to promotion and protection of all human rights around the globe. But some countries have attempted to mislead the agenda of HRC with their political and false narratives. I think it is unfair and even cruel to 12.5 million Xinjiang women whose efforts to fight for their rights as members of modern society haven't been seen. I wish people around the world can pay more attention to Xinjiang and discover the other and true side of women in Xinjiang. The author is a lecturer at School of Politics and lnternational Relations, Lanzhou University. The Times (London) May 14, 2021 Friday Edition 1, Ireland Copyright 2021 Times Newspapers Limited All Rights Reserved Section: NEWS; Pg. 26 Length: 273 words Byline: Louise Watt Body The birthrate in the western Chinese region of Xinjiang, home to many of the country's repressed Uighur minority, has almost halved amid accusations of genocide. Such an extreme decline is unprecedented in the 71 years that the UN has been collecting global fertility statistics, and is larger than the fall in the birthrate during the Syrian civil war and the genocides in Rwanda and Cambodia, according to Nathan Ruser, one of the authors of a report by the Australian Strategic Policy Institute, a think tank. "Our analysis builds on previous work and provides compelling evidence that Chinese government policies in Xinjiang may constitute an act of genocide," the report said. It concluded that China was "cracking down on the reproductive rights" of Uighur and other mostly Muslim minorities in Xinjiang "over perceived fears of instability and uneven" population growth. The research used the Chinese government's statistics to determine that birthrates in Xinjiang fell 49 per cent between 2017 and 2019. The largest declines were in counties where Uighurs and other largely Muslim minority communities are concentrated. The authorities check Uighur and other minority women in Xinjiang to see if they are pregnant, and have forced them to fit intrauterine devices to prevent pregnancy and to undergo sterilisation and abortion, according to research by Adrian Zenz, a German anthropologist, and the Associated Press. On Wednesday human rights groups and western nations led by Britain, the United States and Germany accused China of crimes against Uighurs and demanded that it allow UN experts into Xinjiang. China denies the accusations.
